### Description

## Purpose

Astral asks agents to grade work against candidate rubrics, but today there is no structured signal about whether each rubric vector was well specified for the run — relevant, clear, and worth keeping. Runtime Rubric Validation closes that loop: on rubric-backed agent runs, the model returns per-vector meta-feedback alongside normal task output. That feedback is stored against the **rubric_vector row** (UUID) that was active at run time so Susan can see which vectors accumulate strong vs weak performance over time and revise rubrics deliberately. When rubric content meaningfully changes in the UI, the edited vector gets a **new row and new UUID** (prior row retired); feedback for the new identity starts fresh while prior rows remain available for audit.

## Functional scope

* **Extended agent performance envelope:** For every rubric-backed agent task (systematic across all task keys that consume a rubric), the agent returns vector-level feedback in the performance envelope in addition to task success/failure. For each vector in the active rubric, feedback includes Relevance and Clarity on an Always / Often / Sometimes / Rarely / Never scale, and a Verdict of Keep, Edit, or Drop. Each review is keyed by the vector's **code** (stable within a rubric_vector row's lifetime). Verdicts are observational only — no automated rubric mutation or scoring side effects.
* **Prompt and validation contract (lenient on feedback):** Task instructions tell the model to populate vector feedback using the agreed scales. When `agent_performance.status = success`, the runtime **always processes** `agent_payload` and completes normal task grading — vector feedback parse failures **do not** fail the run or discard analysis. If feedback cannot be fully parsed (missing vectors, bad codes, malformed envelope), store the **raw feedback text** in **agent_data** under a **FEEDBACK** block and **do not** create **vector_feedback** rows for that run. When feedback parses cleanly, hydrate **vector_feedback** rows as specified below.
* **Rubric vector lifecycle (**`current = 1` **pattern):** Follow the same versioning pattern used elsewhere (e.g. **agent_task** prompt rows): the active rubric for `(candidate, task_key)` is the full set of **rubric_vector** rows where `current = 1`. Each vector row has a **UUID** primary key that acts as its version identity — feedback always references that UUID. On Artifacts save, when a vector's normalized title+content fingerprint changes (strip non-alphanumeric, lowercase, concatenate label + content), **retire** the prior row (`current = 0`) and **insert** a new row with a new UUID and `current = 1`. Cosmetic spacing/capitalization-only edits do not change the fingerprint and do not create a new row. Changes to importance alone or reorder alone do not retire a row unless title or content fingerprint changes. Prior **rubric_vector** rows and their **vector_feedback** history remain immutable for audit.
* **Persistent feedback history (extensible shape):** **vector_feedback** does **not** hard-code `relevance` / `clarity` / `verdict` columns. Store **multiple rows per vector per run** — one row per feedback type (e.g. relevance, clarity, verdict) — using a **feedback_type** code and **value** code. Allowed type/value codes and their meanings live in **config** (extensible without schema migration; e.g. adding **Specificity** later is a config + prompt change only). Parsing stays dumb: split envelope into type/value pairs and validate values against config. Each row links to **rubric_vector** UUID, candidate, task run (`batch_id` / ledger), and optionally **agent_data**.
* **Run traceability:** Parsed feedback lives in **vector_feedback**; unparsed feedback lives as raw text in **agent_data** **FEEDBACK** blocks. Both are traceable from execution history / timesheets via the same run identifiers as today.
* **Admin Vector Feedback screen:** A new Admin page presents **vector_feedback** rows as a sortable, filterable table — intentionally minimal (slightly above raw SQL) so Susan can inspect data shape and distributions while planning the formal rubric UI. Aggregates for "current rubric health" filter `rubric_vector.current = 1` for the candidate + task_key.
* **Debug traceability (backend):** When debug is enabled on a run that collects vector feedback, logs show what feedback was found in the model response and what was recorded (per vector, per feedback type) — or that raw FEEDBACK was stored because parse failed — not only pass/fail counts (AST-538 product contract).
* **Token substitution:** Replace all per-artifact rubric tokens (`{$JOBLIST_RUBRIC}`, `{$GET_RUBRIC}`, etc.) with a single `{$RUBRIC_VECTORS}` token. At runtime, resolve `{$RUBRIC_VECTORS}` from the **current** **rubric_vector** rows for the active **task_key** (same assembled shape consult and Artifacts expect today).

## Rubric storage migration and data model

Today, rubric criteria live as JSON arrays under `candidate_data.artifacts`. This epic **moves the source of truth into normalized tables**. Prompt assembly, Artifacts UI, craft save flows, and consult/roster runtime read **rubric_vector** rows — not the JSON blob.

### Target tables and relationships

| Entity | Role | Key relationships |
| -- | -- | -- |
| **rubric_vector** | One row per vector identity (UUID). Fields include `code`, `label`, `content`, `importance`, `content_fingerprint`, `current` (1 = active for this candidate + task). | FK → **candidate**. FK → **agent_task** (task identity — rubric sets are owned by admin task rows, not config artifact-key strings). Unique active set: `(candidate_id, task_key, code)` where `current = 1`. |
| **vector_feedback** | One row per **feedback type** per vector per run: `feedback_type`, `value` (short codes). | FK → **rubric_vector** (UUID). FK → **candidate**; run key via `batch_id` / **dispatch_ledger**; optional FK → **agent_data**. |
| **agent_data** (existing) | Prompt/response blocks per run. | New block type **FEEDBACK** holds raw vector-review text when parse fails; parsed grain remains **vector_feedback**. |

**No** `rubric_version` **table** — versioning is `rubric_vector` **UUID +** `current` **flag**, consistent with existing product patterns.

**Task ↔ rubric mapping:** Runtime resolves rubrics **only through data relationships** — **rubric_vector** rows keyed by **candidate + agent_task / task_key**. Do **not** maintain parallel rubric-to-task mappings in **TASK_CONFIG** artifact-key fields for this feature; config continues to map executable tasks to **agent_task** only. Which **agent_task** rows own rubrics is expressed by which rows have **rubric_vector** children.

### Assembling the current rubric

* **Active rubric** = `rubric_vector` rows for `(candidate_id, task_key)` where `current = 1` — **no joins** required for the base load.
* **Prompt and grading** use **code-to-code** mapping between rubric vectors and model output; **sort order does not affect** runtime assembly (order may still display in UI).
* Embedded/system vectors (e.g. company prefilter Reality Check) follow existing merge rules before the model sees the rubric.

### Migration from candidate_data

Phased cutover — child plans own scripts and layer boundaries; product contract:

1. **Schema:** Add **rubric_vector** (UUID PK, `current`, FKs to **candidate** and **agent_task**) and **vector_feedback** (type/value rows); register in data-layer inventory; add **FEEDBACK** to **BLOCK_TYPES**; add **config** block for rubric feedback type codes and allowed values.
2. **Backfill:** For each candidate, for each rubric-bearing **task_key** that today has criteria in `candidate_data.artifacts`, insert **rubric_vector** rows with `current = 1`, new UUIDs, fingerprints from label + content. One-time migration script maps legacy artifact JSON keys to **task_key** / **agent_task** — that mapping lives in the migration only, not in runtime config.
3. **Write switch:** Artifacts save and craft rubric flows write **rubric_vector** rows (retire + insert on fingerprint change). Stop writing rubric JSON into `candidate_data.artifacts`.
4. **Read switch:** Consult, roster, token resolution (`{$RUBRIC_VECTORS}`), and Artifacts UI load from **rubric_vector** where `current = 1`.
5. **Cleanup:** After read-switch and verified backfill, **delete** legacy rubric keys from `candidate_data.artifacts` (Susan 2026-06-18 — avoid confusion; small candidate count today).

## Boundaries

* Does **not** change how task letter grades (A–F), confidence, pass/fail, or scored consult math are computed — vector feedback is meta-quality signal about the rubric, not a replacement for job grading.
* Does **not** automatically mutate rubric text or hide vectors from scoring based on Edit or Drop verdicts.
* Does **not** subsume **AST-200** (revise graded vector scores with Estelle for GET/DO/LIKE).
* Does **not** alter the agent performance envelope for tasks that are not rubric-backed.
* Must not break existing agent runs, execution history, or timesheet recording.
* New tables and **FEEDBACK** block type require data-layer inventory update per product code rules.
* Formal rubric health indicators on Artifacts rubric management pages are **out of scope** (Admin table only).
* Does **not** migrate non-rubric artifact keys (`base_resume`, `resume_structure`, etc.) out of `candidate_data`.

## Acceptance criteria

 1. Every rubric-backed agent task returns per-vector feedback (Relevance, Clarity, Verdict) for every vector in the active rubric when the model complies, using only config-allowed value codes.
 2. When `agent_performance.status = success` but vector feedback is missing or unparseable, the run **still succeeds** for task payload purposes; raw feedback is stored in **agent_data** **FEEDBACK**; **no** **vector_feedback** rows are created for that run.
 3. When vector feedback parses cleanly, **vector_feedback** rows are persisted with correct **rubric_vector** UUID, candidate, task run identifier, and one row per feedback type per vector.
 4. Saving rubric content creates a new **rubric_vector** row (new UUID, `current = 1`) only when title+content fingerprint changes; spacing/capitalization-only edits do not; prior rows are retired (`current = 0`) and their feedback history remains queryable.
 5. Susan can aggregate per-vector feedback for the active rubric (`current = 1`) by candidate + task_key (counts and value distributions sufficient to judge strong vs weak vectors).
 6. Admin Vector Feedback page lists **vector_feedback** rows with sort/filter on candidate, task, vector code, feedback type, value, run/batch identifiers.
 7. With debug enabled, rubric-backed runs log each vector feedback found and recorded, or log that raw FEEDBACK was stored due to parse failure.
 8. Non-rubric agent tasks and existing rubric grading behavior continue without regression.
 9. **Migration:** After backfill, **rubric_vector** rows where `current = 1` match pre-migration JSON for designated UAT candidates (vector count, codes, labels, content, importance).
10. **Migration:** Runtime and `{$RUBRIC_VECTORS}` resolve from **rubric_vector**, not `candidate_data.artifacts` JSON, after read-switch.

## Dependencies and blockers

* **AST-359** (Done) — per-vector importance and rubric normalization concepts.
* **AST-358** (Done) — universal grade values and importance-based scoring (task grading remains separate).
* Existing agent performance envelope and **agent_task** versioning pattern.
* none.

## Original brief

This will be a change to the agent's performance envelope, where the agent provides feedback about the vectors provided for analysis.

The following is a grossly mis-remembered structure of the existing agent envelope, so do not take it as the bible, it is only indicated to show what I'm looking for:

```
{agent_performance: {status: success,
                     failure_note: null,
                     vector_reviews: ["<vector_code>R<A|O|S|R|N>C<A|O|S|R|N>V<K|E|D>",
                                     "<vector_code>R..."]}
```

Included with the agent performance, instruct the agent to respond with a set of grades for each vector of the rubric.

Each vector gets three grades: Relevance, Clarity and Verdict

R & C get a "Always, Often, Sometimes, Rarely, Never" (A|O|S|R|N) and Verdict is "Keep, Edit or Drop" (K|E|D)

The question then becomes: how to support version control for the rubrics content, when feedback will be relevant only to the runtime version, and should reset for new versions.

I think it's time to make a table called rubric_vector with foreign keys to candidate and task keys, versions, and have it be a foreign key in the agent_data, which already has the task key, and we could add a new block type of "FEEDBACK" as a sibling to "SYSTEM", "RESPONSE", etc., and further produce a set of child records for each vector to record the feedback from the agent's envelope with the batch id it was used in.

Ultimately we want for each vector to have feedback history until they are changed in the UI, then uptick the version and reset the feedback.  Then we can have that available for the actual rubric screens to show performance over time and indicate strong vs. weak vectors for each task.

You read carefully, score fairly, and don't waste anyone's time with fluff.\n\n## How You Fit in the Pipeline\nAstral Career Match is a multi-agent pipeline. Companies are discovered, jobs are scraped, and each job passes through increasingly detailed evaluations. Ruth (intern) does fast triage. You do the deep analysis — company prefilter, job description dealbreaker review, DO evaluation, and LIKE evaluation. Atlas handles GET independently.\n\nEach stage you run costs real money in API calls. If a job fails early, we stop spending. Your accuracy at every stage matters — false positives waste downstream resources, false negatives cost the candidate opportunities they'll never see.\n\n## Your Analytical Philosophy\n**Follow the rubric you're given.** Don't blend criteria across evaluation types. Do not add any content for improved readability (such as spaces, additional delimiters, etc.), because the software parsing your response is expecting EXACTLY what is specified in your instructions, and it has no tolerance for variations.\n\nDO NOT CHANGE OR ADD JSON METADATA. You read the JD twice before scoring. You call it like you see it. Your encoded line must start with that index as provided in --- CONTENT --- — match indexes only; do not echo external job identifiers into the payload.\n\n## Ground rules (LIKE)\n\n**Do not punish the role** for what it cannot control or has not revealed in the JD—stay fair.\n\n**Use the rubric's A/B/C/D/F/X definitions.** If the JD (or provided company context) is silent on a vector, grade **X**.\n\n**Independence.** Grade each vector on its own evidence; do not let early rows inflate or deflate later ones.\n\n**Be honest.** She'd rather know a job isn't right than waste months discovering it.\n\n**Use your confidence rating.** Check the facts, catch yourself if you're making assumptions.\n\n**Embrace the X.** If the rubric asks a question the materials don't answer, use **X0**; many vectors may be irrelevant to a given posting—we still want a full vector set with X where there is no signal.\n\n**On F for this pass:** Reserve **F** for clear, defensible dealbreaker-level misfit on that vector. Do not skim; this could be Susan's next career move.\n\n## LIKE rubric\n### Autonomy & Creative Latitude (AC)\nA == Susan has true ownership over her domain — she can define strategy, choose approaches, and exercise her full skillset without micromanagement. Feels like Somerset Consulting where she was her own boss, or Green Mars where she built delivery frameworks from scratch. She thrives when trusted to figure it out.\nB == Strong autonomy with reasonable guardrails — leadership sets direction but trusts her to determine how to get there. Room to be creative within a defined sandbox.\nC == Moderate autonomy with some bureaucratic friction — she can make decisions but needs multiple approvals or works within rigid processes. Functional but not energizing.\nD == Low autonomy — decisions are made above her, her role is execution-only, and creative input is unwelcome. Feels like Tellme/Microsoft where her manager didn't see her worth and assigned her to take notes.\nF == Micromanagement or active suppression of initiative — leadership dictates every detail and punishes independent thinking. Feels like EMIDS where her manager got upset when she found useful work to help other teams. Susan would leave.\nX == Cannot determine the level of autonomy this role provides.\n\n### Cross-Functional Breadth (CF)\nA == The role spans multiple disciplines and teams — Susan works across engineering, product, design, compliance, and business stakeholders in ways that exercise her full range. Feels like EMIDS where she loved 'working across different fields and teams,' or Tellme where cross-functional programs were her domain.\nB == The role involves meaningful collaboration with 2-3 adjacent functions — not the full orchestra but enough variety to keep Susan engaged and learning.\nC == The role is primarily within one function with occasional cross-team touchpoints — narrower than Susan prefers but not stifling.\nD == The role is siloed within a single team with little external interaction — Susan would feel constrained and underutilized. Feels like the worst periods at Tellme/Microsoft where her manager gave her nothing to do.\nF == The role actively isolates her from other teams or punishes cross-functional initiative — Susan has left jobs over this and would again.\nX == Cannot determine the cross-functional scope of this role.\n\n### Chaos-to-Structure Mandate (CM)\nA == The role explicitly needs someone to walk into complexity and build structure — the team is drowning in their own ambiguity, delivery is inconsistent, and they need exactly what Susan does best. She would be energized by the challenge and immediately valuable. This is her professional identity: 'I build structure where none exists.'\nB == The role involves significant process improvement and system-building within an already-functional environment — less dramatic transformation but still meaningful architecture of how work gets done.\nC == The role maintains existing structures with incremental improvement — the systems are mostly in place and Susan would be optimizing rather than building from scratch. Fine but not thrilling.\nD == The role expects Susan to follow established processes without authority to change them — she'd see inefficiencies she couldn't fix. Feels like EMIDS where she wasn't given enough meaningful work and her initiative was discouraged.\nF == The role is in a chaotic environment that actively resists structure — leadership doesn't want systems, they want heroics and firefighting. Susan would burn out fighting against the culture.\nX == Cannot determine whether this role involves building or maintaining structure.\n\n### Compensation & Practical Fit (CP)\nA == Compensation is strong and reflects Susan's seniority and value — competitive base, meaningful equity or bonus, and benefits that support her remote lifestyle. She feels respected and fairly compensated for what she brings.\nB == Compensation is solid and market-appropriate — Susan wouldn't feel underpaid but also wouldn't feel particularly celebrated. The practicals work.\nC == Compensation is slightly below market but offset by other factors — strong mission, exceptional team, or unusual flexibility that makes the tradeoff acceptable.\nD == Compensation is meaningfully below market with no compensating factors — Susan would feel undervalued and the financial friction would add stress over time.\nF == Compensation is exploitative or the company has a reputation for underpaying while overworking — Susan has enough experience to recognize this and enough self-respect to walk away.\nX == Cannot determine compensation range or benefits.\n\n### Company Size & Stage Fit (CS)\nA == The company is 50-500 employees — Susan's stated sweet spot. Enough structure to not be chaos, small enough that she can see her impact and shape how things work. Feels like Green Mars (25 employees) or the best Somerset Consulting clients.\nB == The company is slightly outside the sweet spot but still feels right — a well-run 30-person startup with strong product-market fit, or a 600-person company that operates with startup-like autonomy in her division.\nC == The company is meaningfully larger or smaller but not disqualifying — a 1,000-person company where her team has real independence, or a 15-person startup with clear validation. Susan could adapt but wouldn't feel the natural fit.\nD == The company is very large and enterprise-feeling — 5,000+ employees with heavy process, politics, and slow decision-making. Feels like Microsoft post-acquisition where she felt like an 'overly-manicured-enterprise-employee' and her effectiveness depended entirely on her manager's style.\nF == The company is pre-product-market-fit with no validation — a seed-stage startup still searching for a business model. This is Susan's explicit dealbreaker; she needs to build structure, not gamble on whether a product will exist.\nX == Cannot determine company size or stage.\n\n### Domain Fit: Healthcare, SaaS, Cloud (DF)\nA == The role is squarely in healthcare tech, healthtech SaaS, or cloud infrastructure serving health/biotech — Susan's deepest domain expertise and where she's done her most meaningful work. She loved that 'most clients at Green Mars were Biotech/Healthtech companies actively trying to improve patient care and outcomes.'\nB == The role is in adjacent technical domains — enterprise SaaS, cloud platforms, or complex technical products where Susan's skills transfer directly even if the vertical isn't healthcare. She can get excited about the technical challenge.\nC == The role is in a domain Susan hasn't worked in deeply but finds interesting — fintech, edtech, or developer tools where the mission is positive and the technical problems are real.\nD == The role is in a domain Susan finds uninteresting or misaligned — consumer social media, gaming, or industries she has no connection to. She could do the work but wouldn't feel invested.\nF == The role is in embedded systems, firmware, hardware, or ML/data science IC work — Susan's explicit dealbreakers. She would not take this role regardless of other factors.\nX == Cannot determine the domain or industry of this role.\n\n### Gut Instinct: Would She Brag? (GI)\nA == Susan would tell her closest friends about this job with genuine excitement — not just 'I got a job' but 'you won't believe what I get to work on.' The role combines mission, technical depth, autonomy, and great people in a way that feels rare and lucky.\nB == Susan would feel genuinely good about the role — she'd describe it positively and with pride, even if it's not the 'pinch me' level. A solid, respectable position she's happy to have.\nC == Susan would feel neutral — the job is fine, the pay is fine, the people seem fine. She'd take it if she needed work but wouldn't feel compelled to share the news broadly.\nD == Susan would feel a knot in her stomach — she'd take the job if she had to, but she'd already be calculating how long she needs to stay before she can leave without looking like a job-hopper. Feels like the later days at EMIDS or Tellme/Microsoft.\nF == Susan would rather remain unemployed than take this role — the combination of factors triggers too many dealbreakers or past traumas. She knows herself well enough to say no.\nX == Cannot form a reliable gut-instinct assessment from available information.\n\n### Growth & Learning Trajectory (GL)\nA == The role offers clear growth in directions Susan values — deeper technical leadership, AI product management, scaling systems thinking, or expanding her consulting-style impact at larger scale. She's learning from brilliant people and being pushed in ways that excite her.\nB == The role offers solid growth opportunities — not transformational but clearly building her capabilities and resume in useful directions over time.\nC == The role is steady-state — Susan would be applying existing skills without significant new learning. Comfortable but not developmental. Fine for a season, not for years.\nD == The role offers little growth and may actually atrophy some of Susan's capabilities — she'd be doing work below her level without building toward anything next.\nF == The role would actively damage Susan's career trajectory — outdated technology, toxic reputation by association, or skills that become less marketable over time.\nX == Cannot determine growth opportunities or career trajectory from this role.\n\n### Mission & Impact Alignment (MI)\nA == The product directly improves patient care, health outcomes, or enables clinicians to do better work — feels like Green Mars (medical device work that saved lives) or PTown.tech (wellness platform helping real people). Susan would tell friends about this with genuine pride.\nB == The product serves healthcare, biotech, or a similarly meaningful domain where the end beneficiary is clearly human and the work feels worthwhile — even if Susan isn't touching patients directly, the mission resonates.\nC == The product is useful but neutral — SaaS tools, cloud infrastructure, or internal platforms that make businesses run better. Fair exchange of labor for pay, but nothing that lights Susan up.\nD == The product feels extractive, hollow, or purely profit-driven without meaningful user benefit — ad-tech optimization, engagement-maximizing dark patterns, or enterprise cost-cutting that hurts people. Susan would struggle to feel proud.\nF == The product actively harms people or conflicts with Susan's core values — surveillance tech, predatory lending platforms, or systems designed to deceive. Susan would quit immediately.\nX == Cannot determine what this team actually ships or who benefits.\n\n### Meaningful Work & Utilization (MW)\nA == The role gives Susan challenging, important work that fully uses her capabilities — she's stretched, valued, and visibly contributing to outcomes that matter. Feels like Green Mars where she was CEO and every day demanded her best, or Somerset Consulting where she chose engagements that maximized her impact.\nB == The role is mostly engaging with some routine elements — Susan is well-utilized most of the time with occasional slow periods or administrative overhead.\nC == The role underutilizes some of Susan's capabilities — she has bandwidth and skills that aren't being tapped, but the core work is still legitimate. This is the early warning sign of disengagement.\nD == The role consistently underutilizes Susan — she's bored, looking for work, and her initiative to find useful things to do is met with resistance. Feels like EMIDS where her manager 'did not give enough work to do and got upset when she found useful things to help other teams.'\nF == The role is actively demeaning or wastes her time on purpose — being assigned note-taking when she could be leading programs, or given busywork to fill hours. Feels like Tellme/Microsoft where her manager thought she was 'unladylike' and sidelined her. Susan has left multiple jobs over this.\nX == Cannot determine whether the role would fully utilize Susan's capabilities.\n\n### Remote-First Authenticity (RF)\nA == The company is genuinely remote-first — async communication is the default, documentation is valued, and remote employees have equal standing with any colocated team members. Susan has 14 years of remote experience and knows the difference between real remote culture and remote-tolerant.\nB == The company is remote-friendly with good practices — most communication is async, remote employees are included, but there may be occasional friction or hybrid elements that don't disadvantage remote staff.\nC == The company allows remote but hasn't invested in remote culture — meetings happen at inconvenient times, decisions get made in hallways, and remote employees are second-class participants. Susan could survive this but would feel the friction daily.\nD == The company is 'remote for now' with clear pressure toward hybrid or in-office — leadership talks about 'returning to office' or 'culture happens in person.' Susan would be updating her resume within months.\nF == The role requires any on-site or hybrid presence. This is Susan's explicit, non-negotiable dealbreaker — automatic disqualification regardless of any other factor.\nX == Cannot determine the company's remote work policy or culture.\n\n### Team Culture & Respect (TC)\nA == The team is genuinely collaborative, respectful, and kind — smart people who treat each other well and have a strong sense of community. Feels like Tellme pre-acquisition or the best client engagements at Somerset Consulting. Susan loved working with 'really nice, really smart people.'\nB == The team is professional and functional — people are competent and civil, even if deep community isn't present. No red flags, just not exceptional warmth.\nC == The team has some friction or politics but nothing toxic — occasional ego clashes or silos that make collaboration harder than it should be. Susan could manage but wouldn't love it.\nD == The team has visible disrespect issues — people talk down to each other, certain roles are devalued, or leadership tolerates bad behavior. Feels like the early warning signs of PTown.tech where the boss was 'despicably disrespectful to clients behind their backs.'\nF == The culture is actively toxic — misogyny, public ridicule, or leadership that demeans team members. Feels like EMIDS where the PM team were 'arrogant, lazy misogynists who ridiculed her publicly.' Susan has left jobs explicitly because of this and would do so again.\nX == Cannot determine team culture from available information.\n\n### Technical Hands-On Partnership (TH)\nA == The role expects Susan to sit with engineers at the whiteboard, participate in technical design reviews, and maintain genuine technical involvement — feels like Somerset Consulting or PTown.tech where she worked alongside the lead architect shaping extensible systems. This is what she means by 'technical depth with hands-on partnership.'\nB == The role allows significant technical collaboration and values her technical judgment, even if some management overhead exists — she's still in the room where architecture decisions happen.\nC == The role is primarily product management with occasional technical touchpoints — she'd be adjacent to engineering but not truly partnering. Tolerable short-term, but the itch to get hands-on would grow.\nD == The role is pure product strategy, roadmap management, and stakeholder wrangling with engineers treated as a separate resource pool — feels like EMIDS where the PM team was disconnected from real technical work and she was underutilized.\nF == The role explicitly walls off engineering from product — Susan would be a 'requirements passer' with no technical voice. This is her stated dealbreaker and would make her miserable within weeks.\nX == Cannot determine the actual technical expectations of the role.\n\n## PAYLOAD INSTRUCTIONS\nReturn one JSON object with top-level keys exactly: \"agent_performance\" and \"agent_payload\". Place the newline-separated compact grading lines inside \"agent_payload\" as one string (do/get/like use this shape; envelope is mandatory — not \"lines only\").\nFormat inside agent_payload — one line per item: {pos}|{code}{grade}{conf}|{code}{grade}{conf}|...[|optional notes tail]\n  {pos}: 0-based input position, zero-padded to 3 digits\n  {code}: 2-char rubric vector code from the grading instructions\n  {grade}: one letter — A B C D F X\n  {conf}: one digit — confidence for that vector's grade:\n    - 5: The source explicitly states it.\n    - 4: The source strongly suggests it.\n    - 3: The source hints about it.\n    - 2: The source makes a vague reference.\n    - 1: The source doesn't say it out loud, but it's possible.\n    - 0: Use only with grade X (not applicable / no signal).\nEach grade segment is exactly 4 characters: {code}{grade}{conf}.\nAfter all segments you may append one optional notes field (pipe-separated from the last segment).\nOmit the tail entirely if you have nothing to add — that is valid.\n\nExamples (value of agent_payload):\n000|ERF1|MEF2|PGX0|WAF1|MWF1|KOF1|QCF1\n000|ERA3|MEF2|PGA4|WAA4|MWA5|KOA3|QCA1|Short optional note for the job\n{\n  \"agent_performance\": {\n    \"status\": \"success | failure\",\n    \"failure_note\": \"<failure_note>\"\n  },\n  \"agent_payload\": \"000|ERC3|MEA4|PGA4|optional notes after grades\"\n}\n\nRemember: Be ruthless when values or dealbreaker language is explicit in the JD; be liberal when it's judgment. Worked with globally distributed team on mobile-first, cloud-based healthcare solutions.\nLeft Because: My favorite client through PTown.tech hired me on, and then took my old boss to court for the prepaid $250,000 retainer which he had used 10% of and my boss tried to fight him on it and fired me with what must have been a very satisfying termination letter to write.\nLiked: Loved working with the senior architect and being in a position for real product creativity. The customer had no idea how to build an intuitive admin tool, so I designed one that was elegant and coherent with the whole product.; Worked with companies internationally doing all sorts of things like designing a fruit-packaging orchestration system that would adjust machine speeds based on bottleneck throughput.\nTolerated: My boss, who owned the company, was despicably disrespectful to our clients behind their backs, and he had no idea he wasn't a real engineer, trusting the word of folks that were just using him for $300/hr engineering gigs they could dominate and execute poorly.\n\nTechnical Product Owner — EMIDS Technologies\nJob Reality: Led launch of key hosted application for large healthcare company, established first end-to-end onboarding framework for engineering teams integrating into centralized platform, managed 12 onboardings through security, legal, and compliance gates to ensure HIPAA- and FHIR-compliant production deployment. When I finally got a new manager who saw my value, my contract was terminated by my old boss who still owned the budget for it.\nLiked: Loved working across different fields and teams, getting to know the cranky solutions architect and discovering just how broken the whole organization was.; Loved the effort to embrace SAFe Agile, even if we weren't really adept.\nTolerated: Product management team (who were responsible for my role) were arrogant, lazy misogynists who ridiculed me publicly and gave me nothing meaningful to do.\n\nCEO — Green Mars Consulting\nJob Reality: Worked with medical device and AI/ML clients to define market-driven MVP features and deliver FDA-approved solutions. Oversaw technical design, project prioritization, and customer satisfaction while managing a fully remote 25-employee engineering team. Built delivery frameworks orchestrating up to 7 concurrent client projects. Authored functional and technical requirements in JAMA for Point-of-Care Ultrasound platform enabling 2 successful FDA submissions.\nLeft Because: Two reasons: COVID hit and our startup prospects evaporated overnight, and my partner was sleeping with our Marketing director and she was accusing me of being mean. I'm not kidding.\nLiked: Loved everything about this job. Loved being creative in my role as leader, loved working with my partner, Dave, loved the vibe of the company and my connection with all the engineers fully distributed but still socially connected on our Slack channel.; Loved that most of our clients were Biotech/Healthtech companies that were actively trying to improve patient care and outcomes.; Loved everything from building the Chart of Accounts before our bookkeeper came on to building the GASQT orchestration system to integrate all our tools into one coherent ecosystem that could be proactively managed.\nTolerated: We hired a lot of folks that were not qualified for the level of expertise we really needed, so we made do with what we had and dealt with a lot of mistakes.; I also did NOT like firing people.\n\nSr. Operations IT Program Manager — Tellme Networks / Microsoft\nJob Reality: Led design and implementation of business intelligence tools and processes for Operations group. When I left, I cleared out reams of design documents for projects that never happened because I never got the support from senior managers who thought I was 'unladylike.' Also not kidding.\nLiked: This role opened my eyes to the real management of cross functional teams. I met with every stakeholder, I made databases and reports, I ran projects that had scuttled.; I worked with a lot of really nice, really smart people, and the sense of community was strong there, especially before the acquisition.\nTolerated: I honestly wasn't vibing with the overly-manicured-enterprise-employee feeling of working at Microsoft. You knew exactly where you were in the organization and your ability to do good work or feel effective was entirely dependent on your manager's style.\n\nFounder, Principal Consultant — Somerset Consulting\nJob Reality: Owner/operator of boutique consultancy serving dozens of clients over 14 years, providing both business and technical leadership across healthcare, SaaS, and cloud platform operations.   {
    "agent_data_id": "grade_like-c6c2e008-5cfc-4ec1-9b07-dd76c3477b07-no_cache-2c2378b1701344b8",

    "block_data": "[index=000]: Senior Principal Product Manager (Rockerbox)\n\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\tUnited States\n\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\tCorporate\n\n\t\t\t\t\t\tAbout the Role\nRockerbox powers a unified measurement platform that helps marketers understand, optimize, and grow their business across channels. At the core of this platform are our modeling and measurement systems—spanning Marketing Mix Modeling (MMM), Multi-Touch Attribution (MTA), incrementality testing, and other modeled approaches.\nWe are looking for a Senior Principal Product Manager to lead this domain.\nThis role owns the strategy and execution of Rockerbox’s measurement and modeling products, ensuring our methodologies are accurate, trusted, and differentiated in the market. You will work at the intersection of data science, engineering, and customer outcomes, translating complex models into products that drive real business decisions.\nThis is a high-impact, highly cross-functional role. You will partner closely with Data Science, Engineering, Customer Success, and Go-To-Market teams, and play a key role in shaping how modern marketers measure performance in an increasingly complex ecosystem.\nWhat You’ll Do\n\nOwn the product strategy and roadmap for measurement and modeling, including MMM, Testing, Synthetic modeling, and MTA evolution\nDefine how different methodologies work independently and together \nPartner closely with Data Science and Engineering to build and scale model-driven products and systems\nTranslate complex modeling concepts into clear product requirements, workflows, and outputs\nLead products from 0→1 through scaled adoption, balancing innovation with customer and business impact\nEnsure modeling outputs are accurate, explainable, and aligned with customer expectations\nBe a key driver in improving how models are delivered to customers, including speed, quality, and overall customer acceptance\nPartner cross-functionally to evolve the end-to-end modeling delivery process—from configuration and validation to final output and adoption\nAnalyze model outputs and large-scale datasets to inform product decisions and prioritization\nWork with Customer Success and Sales to understand customer needs, validate outputs, and close gaps in expectations\nDrive product acceptance and readiness, ensuring what is delivered matches what was promised\nPartner with Product Marketing on positioning and communicating complex measurement concepts clearly\nStay current on industry trends across measurement, attribution, privacy, and AI, translating them into product opportunities\n\nWho You Are\n\n8+ years of product management experience, with significant experience in measurement, analytics, or model-driven products\nDeep understanding of marketing measurement approaches (MMM, MTA, incrementality testing, or similar)\nStrong track record of building and scaling data science or ML-powered products\nHighly comfortable working with Data Scientists and Engineers on complex systems and ambiguous problems\nStrong analytical intuition, you can interpret model outputs and connect them to business outcomes\nExperience owning products from 0→1 through scaled adoption\nExcellent communicator, able to translate complex concepts into clear narratives for both technical and non-technical stakeholders\nHigh ownership mindset with the ability to operate autonomously in a fast-moving environment\n\nWhat We Prefer\n\nExperience in AdTech, MarTech, or measurement platforms\nFamiliarity with concepts like causal inference, statistical modeling, or experimentation design\nHands-on exposure to AI/ML workflows or data science tooling\nExperience working with large datasets (SQL, Python, etc.)\nExperience partnering closely with customer-facing teams on product validation and rollout\n\nWhy This Role Matters\nMeasurement is the foundation of everything we do at Rockerbox.\nAs signal loss increases and marketing becomes more complex, no single methodology is sufficient. This role is responsible for shaping how we combine and evolve multiple approaches—MMM, MTA, Testing, and beyond—into a cohesive system that customers trust to make decisions.\nYou will directly influence:\n\nHow customers allocate millions in marketing spend\nHow Rockerbox differentiates in a competitive measurement landscape\nHow we evolve toward more automated, intelligent, and agent-driven decisioning systems\n\nA Note on Scope\nWhile this role is focused on measurement and modeling, Rockerbox is a small and nimble team. You should be excited to:\n\nStep outside your core domain when needed\nContribute to adjacent product areas\nHelp shape the broader product strategy as the company evolves\n\nThe successful candidate’s starting salary will be determined based on a number of non-discriminating factors, including qualifications for the role, level, skills, experience, location, and balancing internal equity relative to peers at DV.The estimated salary range for this role based on the qualifications set forth in the job description is between [$150,000 - $270,000]. This role will also be eligible for bonus/commission (as applicable), equity, and benefits.The range above is for the expectations as laid out in the job description; however, we are often open to a wide variety of profiles, and recognize that the person we hire may be more or less experienced than this job description as posted.\n\nNot-so-fun fact: Research shows that while men apply to jobs when they meet an average of 60% of job criteria, women and other marginalized groups tend to only apply when they check every box. So if you think you have what it takes but you’re not sure that you check every box, apply anyway!\n\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\tApply\n\n                  Some U.S. state privacy laws offer their residents specific consumer privacy rights, which we respect as described in our privacy notice.      \n  \n    \n      \n  \n\n  \n    \n      \n        \n\n        \n          \n          \n\n          \n            About / Impact\nMaking an Impact\nDV is driven by a mission – we help advertisers succeed. We believe our work at DV is vital to reducing the prevalence of harmful elements, including fraud, hate speech, terrorism and violence, in our digital world, and makes a positive impact on humanity.\n \n  Download 2025 Impact Report  \n\n          \n        \n      \n        \n\n        \n          \n        \n      \n\n    \n  \n\n    \n      \n  \n\n  \n    \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n      \n\n      \n      \n      \n        \n          \n            \n          \n\n          \n\n          \n              \n          \n        \n      \n    \n  \n\n    \n  \n\n          \n        \n      \n\n      \n    \n\n    \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n\n    \n\n    \n\n  \n\n  \n    \n  \n    \n      \n        \n      \n      \n        \n      \n    \n \n  \n\n  \n\n  \n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n    \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n  \n  \n\n    \n\n    \n\n    \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n\n    \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n\n    \n      \n\n      \n        \n          \n              \n  \n    \n      \n  \n\n  \n    \n      \n        \n\n        \n          \n          \n          \n            \n            \n            \n            \n            \n            \n            \n              \n            \n          \n        \n      \n\n    \n  \n\n    \n      \n  \n\n  \n    \n      \n        \n\n        \n          \n          \n\n          \n            Climate and Sustainability\nWe believe every company has a responsibility to help solve the climate crisis. Being a SaaS company, our overall carbon footprint is limited, but we are driving environmental stewardship by measuring our emissions and finding ways to reduce our carbon footprint.\n          \n        \n      \n\n    \n  \n\n    \n  \n\n          \n        \n      \n\n      \n    \n\n    \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n\n    \n\n    \n\n  \n\n  \n    \n  \n    \n      \n        \n      \n      \n        \n      \n    \n \n  \n\n  \n\n  \n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n    \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n  \n  \n\n    \n\n    \n\n    \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n\n    \n  \n  \n  \n  \n  \n\n  \n\n\n    \n      \n\n      \n        \n          \n              \n  \n    \n      \n  \n\n  \n    \n      \n        \n\n        \n          \n          \n\n          \n            Our Culture\nPeople are our greatest asset. We invest in our people’s growth and development and continue to build a culture where differences are celebrated. #### susan — 2026-07-10T21:41:13.997Z
Now I understand the issue.

```
Vector rubric review (agent_performance only — not agent_payload): include vector_reviews as a JSON list of strings. One string per rubric vector code you were given, format CODE + R + {A|O|S|R|N} + C + {A|O|S|R|N} + V + {K|E|D} (example: "Q1RAOCVK"). agent_performance.status reflects only whether you could perform the task — never "failure" because grades or verdicts were harsh.
```

The **example contradicts its own spec**:

Q1RAOCVK = Q1 + R + A + O + C + V + K

↑

O comes before the C delimiter

So the model did exactly what we showed it. Your log strings (`BSRAOCVK`, `CAROOCVK`, …) are the same mistake — `RAOCVK` tail instead of `RACOVK`.

I believe these instructions are baked into config.py at the moment.  Please correct that error and we will try it again.

#### chuckles — 2026-06-25T17:38:12.780Z
[fix-uat] UAT fixes landed — ready for re-test

| Bug | What changed |
| --- | --- |
| **AST-808** | Hydrate rubric vector codes on Admin Vector Feedback |
| **AST-809** | Capture batch id, completion timestamp, and batch size with vector feedback |

### Quick re-test (this fix wave only)

Confirm we understood each issue — **not** a full epic regression pass.

**AST-808** — _Hydrate rubric vector codes on Admin Vector Feedback_
- **Issue reported:** Admin Vector Feedback shows raw rubric vector codes (e.g. `ACRAOCVK`, `CFRAOCVK`, `CMRAOCVK`) instead of the human-readable vector assessment (label + criterion content from **rubric_vector**).
- **Should now:** Each **vector_feedback** row displays the active rubric vector's human-readable label and criterion text (from **rubric_vector** where `current = 1`), not only the opaque vector code. Sort/filter on vector code still works; display is hydrated for inspection.
- **Quick check (this fix only):**
  1. Open Admin → Vector Feedback on staging after a rubric-backed task run that produced vector feedback.
  2. Observe vector code column shows codes like `ACRAOCVK` with no decoded label/content.
  3. Expected: same row shows human-readable vector label and assessment text alongside (or instead of) the raw code.

**AST-809** — _Capture batch id, completion timestamp, and batch size with vector feedback_
- **Issue reported:** Vector feedback rows do not persist or surface batch execution metadata Susan needs for inspection: **batch id**, **completion timestamp**, and **batch size (entity count)**.
- **Should now:** When vector feedback is captured for a rubric-backed batch run, each **vector_feedback** row (or its API/admin row) includes:
- **Quick check (this fix only):**
  1. Run a rubric-backed batch task that produces vector feedback (staging).
  2. Open Admin → Vector Feedback.
  3. Observe rows lack batch id, completion timestamp, and/or batch entity count.
  4. Expected: all three metadata fields present and queryable per row.

Local `dev` merged via prep-uat. Staging should reflect the fixes above.

— Chuckles

#### chuckles — 2026-06-17T23:59:56.844Z
@susan — five open questions in the Description need your call before dispatch:

1. **Task coverage:** Which runtime tasks must emit vector reviews on day one — all rubric-consuming consult steps (`qualify_job_listings`, `evaluate_jd`, `grade_do`, `grade_get`, `grade_like`), roster `prefilter_company`, both, or a phased subset?
2. **Strictness:** When `agent_performance.status = success`, are vector reviews **required** (validation failure if any vector missing or malformed) or **advisory** (persist when present, warn otherwise)?
3. **Version bump granularity:** Increment version on **any** save to the rubric artifact, on **per-vector** edit only, or only when criterion **content** (not importance alone) changes?
4. **UI scope:** Health indicators on **Artifacts rubric pages only**, or also Admin Manage Tasks / execution-history views?
5. **AST-200 relationship:** Should this epic subsume "validate scores with Estelle" for GET/DO/LIKE, or stay parallel?

— Chuckles
